  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the introduction of punishment (recipient's power to reject) increases fairness in allocation, particularly among those predisposed to selfishness. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, using dictator and ultimatum games, and the outcome is individual allocation, not group welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can shift behavior toward fairness, but the lack of payoff-based outcomes and the hypothetical, one-shot nature of the games limit its direct predictive value for efficiency in repeated, incentivized PGG-like environments.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A. (2008). The price you pay: cost-dependent reputation effects of altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who invest more in costly third-party punishment are perceived as more fair and trustworthy, and are subsequently entrusted with more money in a trust game. This effect is contingent on the cost being a reliable signal (i.e., known relative to endowment). The effect is mediated by perceptions of fairness, not by general likability or generosity. The study does not measure group efficiency or public goods provision, but rather the reputational and trust consequences for punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide indirect evidence that costly punishment can enhance the punisher's reputation and increase trust from others, but only when the cost is interpretable as a reliable signal. For prediction tasks focused on group efficiency in PGGs, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that punishment may have reputational benefits for the punisher, which could, in theory, affect cooperation and efficiency in repeated or reputation-sensitive environments, but it does not test this directly. The design and outcomes are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Perugini, M., Gallucci, M., Presaghi, F., & Ercolani, A. P. (2003). The personal norm of reciprocity. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that individual differences in positive and negative reciprocity (as measured by the PNR questionnaire) predict how people allocate money to others in response to fair or unfair prior actions, both in hypothetical and incentivized settings. Positive reciprocity predicts greater rewarding of positive behavior, while negative reciprocity predicts greater punishment of negative behavior. These effects are robust across cultures (Italy and UK) and are distinct from general prosocial orientation or Big Five personality traits. However, the studies do not directly examine group efficiency, total welfare, or the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes in repeated public goods or social dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in the propensity to punish or reward (reciprocity norms) influence how people respond to others' actions in allocation games, including costly punishment and reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings are most useful for understanding heterogeneity in punishment/reward behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. The allocation tasks are adjacent to PGGs but lack repeated group interaction, explicit MPCR, or group efficiency measures.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2005). Norm enforcement among the Ju/'hoansi Bushmen - A case of strong reciprocity?. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is a frequent and central mechanism for enforcing social norms among the Ju/'hoansi, with most norm enforcement occurring through criticism, mockery, or coalition-based verbal sanctions rather than material or violent means. The costs of punishment are generally low due to cultural mechanisms that distribute costs, allow restraint, and minimize escalation, but are not negligible. Punishment is more often used than reward, and is primarily aimed at maintaining group harmony and curbing disruptive behavior rather than strictly deterring free-riding. The study finds some support for the strong reciprocity hypothesis, as individuals sometimes incur costs to punish norm violators even when not directly benefiting. However, the outcomes of punishment are mixed, with only a minority of cases resulting in visible reform. The study does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or material payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar experimental settings. Its relevance is primarily in providing ethnographic context and qualitative insight into how punishment operates in a real-world cooperative society, including the social costs, coalition dynamics, and norm enforcement mechanisms. It does not report or allow estimation of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not manipulate or systematically vary game design dimensions. Therefore, it should not be used as a quantitative datapoint for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games, but may inform qualitative understanding of punishment's social functions and costs.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2011). Punishment sustains large-scale cooperation in prestate warfare. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The Turkana sustain costly, large-scale cooperation in warfare through informal punishment of free riders. Raiding parties are large and composed of unrelated individuals from multiple social units. Free riding (desertion, cowardice, unfair loot appropriation) is common, but is often sanctioned through verbal reprimands, corporal punishment, and fines. The system relies on third-party consensus and community enforcement, not formal institutions. Norms governing warfare are group-beneficial at the ethnolinguistic scale, not just for smaller social units. While the paper provides rich evidence on the mechanisms and scale of cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct measures of efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the real-world operation of punishment and its role in sustaining cooperation at large scales, especially in the absence of formal institutions. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a controlled experimental design. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, the paper offers strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation and reduce free riding, even in large, anonymous groups. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the mechanisms and social context of punishment, rather than for parameterizing efficiency outcomes in laboratory PGGs. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective at large group sizes and in the absence of repeated interaction or kinship, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency changes due to punishment in a controlled game setting.

- source: Mulder, L. B., Verboon, P., & De Cremer, D. (2009). Sanctions and moral judgments: The moderating effect of sanction severity and trust in authorities. *European Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that severe sanctions (relative to mild or no sanctions) increase the extent to which people judge rule-breaking as morally wrong, and this effect is moderated by trust in authorities: it is present only when trust is high. In a behavioral allocation task, severe sanctions also increase social disapproval of rule-breakers (willingness to forgo personal gain to disadvantage a rule-breaker), again only when trust in authorities is high. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct economic outcome, and does not use a public goods game or a close variant. The results are relevant for understanding how punishment can shape moral norms and social enforcement, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms (moral judgment, social disapproval) that may underlie compliance and norm enforcement, especially the moderating role of trust in authorities and sanction severity. However, because the outcomes are not payoff-based and the game structure is not a PGG or close variant, the paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for contextual or mechanistic discussion, especially regarding how punishment can affect perceived legitimacy and social norms.

- source: Brandts, J., & Charness, G. (2011). The strategy versus the direct-response method: a first survey of experimental comparisons. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that, across a wide range of experimental games (including some with punishment and cooperation), the method of elicitation (strategy vs. direct response) usually does not significantly affect behavior. However, in games involving punishment, direct response tends to result in higher punishment rates than the strategy method. The paper notes that this difference is robust across several studies, but the effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The survey also finds that differences between methods are more likely when there are fewer contingent choices or in one-shot games, but these effects are not strong. Overall, the strategy method is generally a valid tool for studying behavior, but may understate punishment rates compared to direct response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment. It suggests that the elicitation method can affect observed punishment rates, with direct response yielding higher punishment. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed, the paper does not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that studies using the strategy method may understate the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, on efficiency. When synthesizing evidence, one should be cautious about comparing results across studies using different elicitation methods, especially for punishment-related outcomes.

- source: Balafoutas, L., & Nikiforakis, N. (2012). Norm enforcement in the city: A natural field experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that some individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment to enforce social norms in one-shot, anonymous field interactions, but this is rare (11.7% overall, 4% for non-littering, 19.3% for escalator norm). Men are more likely to punish than women; the violator's height and gender do not significantly affect enforcement rates. The main reason for not punishing is fear of retaliation. The better-known (universal) norm (non-littering) is enforced less often than the environment-specific norm (escalator), despite being more widely valued. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the willingness to engage in costly punishment in real-world, one-shot norm violation settings, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings suggest that, in natural field settings, the rate of costly punishment is much lower than in laboratory public goods games, possibly due to higher perceived costs (e.g., risk of retaliation) and lack of anonymity. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the mere availability of punishment may not translate into high rates of enforcement or efficiency gains in field-like conditions. However, because no group payoff or efficiency data are reported, the paper cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the behavioral limits of punishment in naturalistic settings and the importance of context (e.g., fear of counter-punishment, norm universality) as moderators.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2005). Punishment and partner switching cause cooperative behaviour in a cleaning mutualism. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that cleaner fish, when exposed to simulated punishment (chasing) or partner switching (fleeing) after eating their preferred food, learn to avoid the preferred food and instead eat more of the less preferred food. This behavioral shift is interpreted as evidence that punishment and partner switching can promote cooperative behavior in mutualistic interactions. The control group, which experienced no negative consequences, increased their preference for the preferred food. The findings support the idea that both punishment and the threat of partner switching can be effective partner control mechanisms, but the outcomes are measured in terms of behavioral change rather than direct payoffs or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and partner switching can alter behavior in a way that is consistent with increased cooperation, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, focusing on dyadic mutualism rather than group contributions and payoffs. For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper suggests that punishment and partner switching can change behavior toward more cooperative outcomes, but does not quantify the effect on efficiency or provide data on group-level payoffs. Its main value is in supporting the behavioral mechanism by which punishment can promote cooperation.

- source: Price, M. E., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2002). Punitive sentiment as an anti-free rider psychological device. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals' willingness to participate in a collective action (e.g., military draft) is a strong predictor of their desire to punish nonparticipants (free riders), but not of their desire to reward participants. Perceived self-interest in the group goal predicts pro-reward sentiment but not punitive sentiment, once willingness to participate is controlled for. The results suggest that punitive sentiment is a specialized psychological adaptation for eliminating the fitness advantage of free riders, rather than for recruiting labor or maximizing group efficiency. The study does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a public goods game or similar environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar experimental settings. Its findings are based on survey responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or payoffs. However, it does provide indirect support for the idea that punitive sentiment is psychologically linked to participation in collective action, and that punishment may function to deter free riding. For the prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & McAuliffe, K. (2012). Human punishment is motivated by inequity aversion, not a desire for reciprocity. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that punishment is triggered by disadvantageous inequity rather than by losses alone. When cheating by a partner results in the victim having a lower payoff than the cheat, punishment is common; when cheating only causes a loss but not inequity, punishment is rare. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game or close variant. The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motives for punishment, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the motivation for punishment in one-shot dyadic games is driven by inequity aversion, not by losses per se. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a repeated game structure. Thus, it offers only indirect, mechanistic insight for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. It suggests that punishment may be more likely to occur (and thus potentially affect efficiency) when cheating creates disadvantageous inequity, but does not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ashlock, D., Smucker, M. D., Stanley, E. A., & Tesfatsion, L. (1996). Preferential partner selection in an evolutionary study of Prisoner's Dilemma. *Biosystems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dyadic repeated Dictator Game settings, observers rate those who withdraw cooperation from defectors as more cooperative and less vengeful than those who punish. Both withdrawers and punishers are seen as less exploitable than non-sanctioners, but only when the sanctioner ends up with a positive payoff. Punishers are least preferred as future partners. The reputational consequences of punishment are negative compared to withdrawal, and the effect on perceived exploitability depends on whether the sanctioner retains a positive payoff. No actual efficiency or group payoff is measured, so the findings are about perceptions and reputational inferences, not realized payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about reputational perceptions in dyadic games, not about actual payoffs or efficiency. However, it suggests that punishment may have reputational costs compared to withdrawal of cooperation, and that the effect of sanctions on perceived exploitability depends on whether the sanctioner retains a positive payoff. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the game structure is dyadic, not group-based.

- source: O'Gorman, R., Wilson, D. S., & Miller, R. R. (2005). Altruistic punishing and helping differ in sensitivity to relatedness, friendship, and future interactions. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that participants report a strong desire to punish cheaters at personal cost, regardless of whether the cheater is a relative, friend, or stranger, and regardless of the likelihood of future interaction. In contrast, willingness to help is much more sensitive to relatedness and future interaction. The study does not use real economic games or measure group efficiency or payoffs, but rather relies on self-reported hypothetical willingness to punish or help. The results suggest that the psychological impulse to punish norm violators is less conditional on kinship or reciprocity cues than the impulse to help, at least in hypothetical settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that the psychological willingness to punish is robust to changes in relatedness and future interaction cues, which may imply that, in real games, punishment behavior could be less sensitive to these variables than helping behavior. However, because the study does not use real payoffs, group outcomes, or actual public goods games, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited and indirect.

- source: McCabe, K. A., Rassenti, S. J., & Smith, V. L. (1996). Game theory and reciprocity in some extensive form experimental games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that allowing for direct punishment of defection (game 1) leads to higher cooperation and efficiency compared to when punishment is not available (game 2), though the difference is modest in single-play but more pronounced in repeated interactions. Efficiency (as a percentage of the cooperative joint payoff) is highest in repeated same-pair treatments with punishment (90.6%), and lower in random or single-play treatments (82.6%-86.9%). The presence of punishment increases the expected payoff for cooperative choices and reduces the profitability of defection. The probability of cooperation increases with the likelihood of repeated interaction with the same partner, and punishment is used at nontrivial rates even when costly. Overall, punishment and repeated interaction both have positive effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in extensive form bargaining games with a punishment option, enabling punishment increases efficiency, especially when combined with repeated interaction with the same partner. The efficiency boost from punishment is present even in single-play, but is much larger in repeated games. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence of punishment, the matching protocol (same partner vs random), and the number of rounds. The efficiency values reported (Table 4) can be used as quantitative anchors for similar game designs. The findings support the prediction that, controlling for baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the probability of repeated interaction and the cost/effectiveness of punishment.

- source: Engle-Warnick, J., & Slonim, R. (2006). Inferring repeated-game strategies from actions: evidence from trust game experiments. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study uses a novel inference method to map observed actions in repeated trust games to underlying strategies. In indefinite games (with a continuation probability of 0.8), many subjects use harsh trigger strategies that punish non-cooperation, supporting cooperative outcomes and higher payoffs. Over time, the proportion of play consistent with these strategies increases, and average payoffs move toward the cooperative equilibrium. In finite games (5 rounds), strategies with end-game defection dominate, and payoffs decline toward the non-cooperative equilibrium. The paper provides evidence that punishment-like strategies (grim trigger) are effective in sustaining cooperation and higher payoffs in indefinite games, but not in finite games.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (in the form of harsh trigger strategies) in improving efficiency or payoffs depends strongly on the game horizon: indefinite repetition with sufficient continuation probability enables punishment to sustain cooperation and higher payoffs, while finite repetition leads to unraveling and lower payoffs. The trust game is not a standard PGG, but the findings are likely to transfer to other repeated social dilemmas with similar strategic structure. The presence of punishment strategies is associated with more positive efficiency effects in indefinite games, but not in finite games.

- source: Gurven, M., & Winking, J. (2008). Collective action in action: Prosocial behavior in and out of the laboratory. *American Anthropologist*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that among the Tsimane, offers in the Dictator Game, Ultimatum Game, and Third-Party Punishment Game are low, and punishment (rejection or third-party punishment) is infrequent. There is little to no correlation between individuals' behavior in these games and their real-world prosocial actions such as food sharing, labor for public goods (well construction), or contributions to communal feasts. The results suggest that context and payoff structure are more important than stable individual traits in predicting prosocial behavior. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and thus does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. While it includes a third-party punishment game (TPPG), the outcome measures are individual offers and punishment rates, not group efficiency or payoff. The study does show that punishment is rare and offers are low in this context, suggesting that, at least in this population and game structure, punishment does not play a strong role in promoting prosocial behavior. However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper cannot be used to calibrate or predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

- source: Fudenberg, D., Rand, D. G., & Dreber, A. (2012). Slow to Anger and Fast to Forgive: Cooperation in an Uncertain World. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with noise, cooperation is frequent and yields higher payoffs than defection when the benefit/cost ratio is high enough to support cooperative equilibria. Lenient and forgiving strategies (e.g., Tit-for-2-Tats, Grim2) are both common and successful in these settings. When only defection is an equilibrium (low b/c), defection dominates and is most profitable. The presence of noise increases the use of longer-memory, more lenient strategies. The paper provides detailed payoff matrices and shows that, in treatments with cooperative equilibria, the highest-earning strategies are lenient and forgiving. Social preferences (as measured by a dictator game) do not explain cooperation; strategic concerns do.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with and without noise, especially as a function of the benefit/cost ratio and error rate. While not a public goods game, the repeated PD is closely related, and the findings on how game structure (payoff ratios, noise) affect the efficiency of cooperative vs. noncooperative strategies are directly relevant. The paper provides explicit expected payoffs for each strategy under different conditions, allowing for quantitative prediction of group efficiency. The results show that, when the game supports cooperative equilibria, efficiency is higher and cooperative strategies outperform defectors. The absence of explicit punishment/reward mechanisms means the findings are most relevant for environments where punishment is endogenous (via strategy choice) rather than via a separate punishment stage.

- source: Ellingsen, T., & Johannesson, M. (2004). Promises, threats and fairness. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a one-shot investment/bargaining game, allowing pre-play communication (either a promise from the buyer or a threat from the seller) increases the rate of investment and the mean profit of the investor (seller) compared to no communication. Buyer promises are always kept and lead to the highest investor profits (mean profit +8.75 SEK), while seller threats are less credible and less effective (mean profit -6.43 SEK). Without communication, mean profit is lowest (-12.86 SEK). The study provides direct evidence that communication, especially credible promises, can mitigate efficiency losses in social dilemmas similar to public goods or hold-up problems, even in the absence of formal punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling credible communication (especially promises) between parties in a social dilemma increases efficiency and group payoff, even in a one-shot setting. While the game is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related in structure (investment creates surplus, division is bargained). The findings suggest that, in similar environments, introducing a channel for credible promises (but not necessarily punishment) can substantially improve efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting the effect of communication or commitment devices, rather than punishment per se, but the analysis of threats (costly punishment) is informative: threats are less credible and less effective than promises. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that communication/commitment channels can substitute for formal punishment in improving efficiency, but that the effect depends on the credibility and nature of the commitment (promise vs. threat).

- source: Brown, M., Falk, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). Relational contracts and the nature of market interactions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The absence of third-party enforcement leads to the endogenous formation of long-term firm-worker relationships, where contract renewal is contingent on high effort. This informal punishment mechanism (firing/termination) disciplines workers and results in higher effort, higher wages, and much higher efficiency and surplus compared to settings where such relationships are not possible. The effect is robust: both firms and workers earn more in long-term relationships, and the distribution of surplus is more equal. When long-term relationships are ruled out (random rematching), effort and efficiency drop sharply, and minimal effort becomes common. When third-party enforcement is available, the market behaves competitively, with low wages, low rent sharing, and little incentive for long-term relationships. The findings show that informal, endogenous punishment (via contract renewal/firing) can substitute for formal enforcement and substantially improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated market-like environments with incomplete contracts, the ability to endogenously form long-term relationships and use contract renewal/firing as a sanction leads to much higher efficiency and surplus than settings where such informal punishment is not possible. For prediction tasks, the presence of repeated interactions, the ability to condition future play on past behavior, and the absence of third-party enforcement are key moderators of punishment's positive effect on efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where informal punishment via relationship termination is feasible, efficiency can approach the level seen with formal enforcement, provided that agents can build reputations and relationships. The results are most directly relevant to labor market and relational contract settings, but the underlying mechanism is adjacent to public goods games with endogenous partner choice and repeated play.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schneider, F. (2010). Eyes are on us, but nobody cares: are eye cues relevant for strong reciprocity?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that subtle, implicit reputation cues (eyespots) do not increase trustees' altruistic behavior in a trust game, nor do they affect trustors' willingness to invest. In contrast, explicit pecuniary reputation incentives—where trustees' past back-transfers are revealed to future trustors—lead to a large and significant increase in both trustees' back-transfers and trustors' investments. This effect is particularly pronounced among individuals with high Machiavellianism scores. The results suggest that explicit, material reputation incentives are powerful drivers of prosocial behavior, while implicit cues without real consequences are ineffective in this context. The findings cast doubt on the hypothesis that most prosocial behavior in anonymous experiments is due to uncontrolled implicit reputation cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that explicit, material reputation incentives (a form of positive sanctioning or reward) can substantially increase prosocial behavior and, by implication, group efficiency in trust-based economic games. However, it also shows that implicit cues (such as eyespots) have no such effect. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of explicit reputation or reward mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency, while subtle cues without real payoff consequences are not. The results are adjacent to public goods games: the trust game is a two-player, sequential, one-shot game repeated with random rematching, not a standard PGG, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or reward is at stake, and less so for punishment-based designs. The paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, but supports the importance of explicit incentive structures for efficiency.

- source: Eckel, C. C., & Grossman, P. J. (1996). The relative price of fairness: Gender differences in a punishment game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when subjects can choose to reward a fair partner at a personal cost (i.e., accept a lower own payoff), a substantial fraction do so, especially when the cost is low. Women are more likely than men to sacrifice earnings for fairness when the cost is low, but their willingness drops sharply as the cost increases. Men are less sensitive to the cost and maintain a relatively constant (lower) rate of sacrificing for fairness. The presence of the punishment/reward option leads to lower average payoffs compared to always choosing the higher own-payoff option, indicating a negative effect on efficiency. The study does not use a repeated public goods game or a standard PGG, but the design is adjacent in that it involves costly punishment/reward decisions affecting both own and others' payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the availability of a punishment/reward option (even in a dictator-like setting) can reduce average efficiency, as some subjects are willing to accept lower payoffs to reward fairness or punish unfairness. The effect is moderated by the cost of punishment/reward and by gender. In environments where punishment/reward is costly and voluntary, efficiency may decrease relative to a control with no such option, especially if the cost is not prohibitively high. However, the design is not a repeated PGG, so direct quantitative prediction for standard PGGs should be cautious. The main implication is that costly punishment/reward options can reduce group efficiency, particularly when the cost is low and when participants are more likely to act on fairness preferences.

- source: McCabe, K. A., Rassenti, S. J., & Smith, V. L. (1998). Reciprocity, trust, and payoff privacy in extensive form bargaining. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in two-person extensive form bargaining games, the possibility of punishment (even if not rational in the subgame) increases the frequency of efficient, cooperative outcomes (SJM) compared to games where punishment is not possible. Repeated play with the same partner further increases cooperation and efficiency. When payoff information is private, cooperation and efficiency decline, and noncooperative (SP) outcomes predominate. The paper reports expected payoffs and frequencies of efficient outcomes by treatment, showing that punishment and repeated interaction both have positive effects on group efficiency. However, the effect of punishment is less pronounced than the effect of repeated interaction and complete information. The findings suggest that both punishment opportunities and the ability to build trust/reciprocity are important for achieving high efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While not a standard public goods game, the design is adjacent: it is a two-player extensive form game with opportunities for cooperation, defection, and punishment. The results show that enabling punishment increases the frequency of efficient outcomes and average payoffs, especially in single-play settings. Repeated interaction and complete information further enhance efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency relative to control, but the effect is moderated by information conditions and the possibility of repeated interaction. The paper supports the prediction that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when players can observe each other's payoffs and when repeated interaction is possible.

- source: Wang, J., Suri, S., & Watts, D. J. (2012). Cooperation and assortativity with dynamic partner updating. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that dynamic partner updating—where players can choose to make or break ties with others—substantially increases both cooperation rates and average payoffs in iterated prisoner's dilemma games, compared to static networks. Even low rates of partner updating are sufficient to produce large positive effects. The effect is concave: initial increases in update rate have large effects, but further increases yield smaller gains. When the payoff matrix is modified so that maintaining ties with defectors is more costly, cooperation is even higher and more sustained, as players are more willing to sever ties with defectors. The main mechanism is assortative mixing: cooperators preferentially connect with other cooperators and avoid defectors, rather than punishing defectors directly. The results suggest that dynamic partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment or reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation and increasing group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling dynamic partner updating (i.e., endogenous, bilateral partner choice and unilateral link deletion) in repeated social dilemma games can dramatically increase group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum), even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The effect is robust across a range of update rates and is further enhanced when the cost of interacting with defectors is increased. For prediction tasks, the presence of dynamic partner updating should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially when players can freely choose partners and sever ties. However, the game is an iterated prisoner's dilemma on a dynamic network, not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implicit (via partner selection) rather than explicit. Thus, while the findings are highly informative for environments with endogenous partner choice, they are only adjacent to explicit punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that, in settings where partner choice is possible, explicit punishment may be less necessary for achieving high efficiency.

- source: Rustagi, D., Engel, S., & Kosfeld, M. (2010). Conditional Cooperation and Costly Monitoring Explain Success in Forest Commons Management. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that (1) groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators have significantly better forest management outcomes, (2) costly monitoring (enforcement) is more prevalent in groups with more conditional cooperators, and (3) both conditional cooperation and costly monitoring are important for successful commons management. The main outcome is a real-world efficiency proxy (forest management success), not laboratory earnings. The public goods game is used to measure behavioral types, not as the main outcome. The study does not experimentally manipulate punishment or monitoring, but observes natural variation in monitoring (enforcement) and links it to outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that costly monitoring (enforcement) is associated with higher group-level efficiency in a real-world commons context, and that the presence of conditional cooperators amplifies this effect. While the study does not manipulate punishment in a controlled PGG, it supports the idea that enabling or increasing costly enforcement (monitoring and punishment) can improve group efficiency, especially when conditional cooperators are present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and intensity of enforcement mechanisms (even if costly) are likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, particularly in groups with more conditional cooperators. However, the evidence is observational and context-specific (forest management), not from a lab PGG with toggled punishment.

- source: Wang, R. W., Sun, B. F., Zheng, Q., Shi, L., & Zhu, L. X. (2011). Asymmetric interaction and indeterminate fitness correlation between cooperative partners in the fig-fig wasp mutualism. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric interference competition among cooperative actors (wasps) can stabilize cooperation by keeping resource use unsaturated, leading to positive fitness correlations (mutual benefit) between partners. When interference competition is prevented (sequential entry), or when wasp oviposition efficiency is high (e.g., in cold season), the system can shift to negative fitness correlation (conflict), indicating overexploitation and reduced group efficiency. The relationship between cooperation and efficiency is not fixed but varies stochastically with environmental and experimental factors. The paper also discusses the role of discriminative sanctions (host reducing egg deposition efficiency of less-cooperative wasps) and rewards in maintaining cooperation, but these are not directly manipulated as in PGG punishment/reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that mechanisms analogous to punishment (sanctions by the host) and interference competition among cooperators can maintain or undermine group efficiency, depending on ecological context and the structure of interaction. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of resource competition, the number of actors, and environmental conditions. However, the system studied is a biological mutualism, not a PGG or direct human social dilemma, and the 'punishment' is not implemented as a discrete, costly action by peers but as host-imposed sanctions. Thus, while the results are adjacent and informative, they should be transferred to PGG predictions with caution, especially regarding the direct effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.

- source: Abbink, K., & Sadrieh, A. (2009). The pleasure of being nasty. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of subjects are willing to destroy the other player's endowment when their actions are hidden, but not when they are observable. The presence of anonymity increases anti-social behavior. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods or punishment game. The findings are relevant for understanding anti-social punishment and spite, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The game studied is a two-player, anti-social destruction game with no cooperation or group payoff structure. The main insight is that anonymity increases anti-social behavior, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The findings may be contextually relevant for understanding anti-social punishment or spiteful behavior, but do not inform the prediction task in a quantitative or directly comparable way.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2009). Avoiding the sharp tongue: Anticipated written messages promote fair economic exchange. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The opportunity for receivers to send ex post written messages (expressing disapproval or emotion) in a one-shot dictator game leads to a statistically significant reduction in the frequency of maximally selfish (10%) offers by dictators, compared to the standard dictator game. However, the frequency of fair (50/50) offers is not significantly increased. The effect of non-monetary sanctions (messages) is smaller than that of monetary punishment (rejection) in ultimatum games, which more strongly reduce selfish offers but at the cost of efficiency due to destroyed surplus. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, as the dictator game does not involve group production or a public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal, non-monetary sanctions (written messages) on selfishness in a simple allocation game, but not in a public goods or group production context. It shows that non-monetary sanctions can reduce selfish behavior, but less so than monetary punishment. However, because the game is a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated or multi-player public goods game), and efficiency is not measured, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that in environments where only non-monetary sanctions are possible, some reduction in selfishness may occur, but the effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be directly inferred from this study.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Klapwijk, A., & Van Munster, L. M. (2011). How the shadow of the future might promote cooperation. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the mere anticipation of repeated interaction (10 rounds vs. 1 round) increases cooperation (measured as coins given to the partner) among individualists, but not among prosocials, in a sequential give-some dilemma. Prosocials maintain high cooperation regardless of repetition, while individualists reciprocate more when repeated interaction is expected. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of 'noise' in the translation of choices. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that repeated interaction (longer games) can increase cooperation among individualistically oriented participants, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. However, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or include punishment, so its relevance to the prediction of efficiency under punishment is limited. The main implication is that repeated interaction may substitute for some effects of punishment among individualists, but this is not tested in a payoff or efficiency framework.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are rational maximizers in an ultimatum game. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees, when tested in a mini-ultimatum game, do not systematically reject unfair offers, even when a fair alternative is available to the proposer. Proposers do not make fair offers, and responders accept almost all nonzero offers, only reliably rejecting offers where they receive nothing. This contrasts with human behavior, where unfair offers are often rejected as a form of costly punishment. The findings suggest that chimpanzees are rational maximizers and do not engage in costly punishment to enforce fairness. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses to fairness and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a mini-ultimatum game with chimpanzees and focuses on behavioral outcomes (offer choices, rejection rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative in showing that, in the absence of a willingness to punish unfairness (i.e., when punishment is not exercised), there is no mechanism to enforce fair or cooperative outcomes. This suggests that, in environments where punishment is not used or is not costly, efficiency gains from punishment are unlikely. The findings are adjacent to the prediction task and may inform models about the necessity of actual punishment behavior for efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2002). Crime in the lab-detecting social interaction. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates strong social interaction effects: subjects' willingness to steal increases with the observed or expected stealing of others. About half of the subjects are 'reciprocal' (conditional) types, while the rest are mostly selfish (always steal the maximum). The fraction of selfish types is higher when stealing is a pure transfer (no efficiency loss) than when it is inefficient. The study does not include a punishment or sanctioning phase, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding norm compliance and conditional cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment or on efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about the importance of social interaction and conditional norm violation: when individuals see more norm violation (stealing), they are more likely to violate norms themselves. The manipulation of the efficiency consequences of stealing (gamma) shows that more selfish behavior occurs when norm violation is less costly to the group, which may be relevant for understanding the impact of efficiency parameters in related games. However, because there is no punishment mechanism and no direct efficiency or payoff analysis, this paper should be considered only as indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Rilling, J. K., Goldsmith, D. R., Glenn, A. L., Jairam, M. R., Elfenbein, H. A., Dagenais, J. E., Murdock, C. D., & Pagnoni, G. (2008). The neural correlates of the affective response to unreciprocated cooperation. *Neuropsychologia*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that unreciprocated cooperation (being defected on after cooperating) in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma elicits strong negative emotions and increased neural activity in regions associated with aversive and social pain (anterior insula, hippocampus, amygdala). These neural responses are stronger when the unreciprocated cooperation follows a cooperative move by the subject, suggesting sensitivity to perceived partner intent. Functional connectivity between the anterior insula and lateral orbitofrontal cortex predicts whether the subject will defect in the next round. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma games. It does, however, provide evidence that unreciprocated cooperation (partner defection) triggers strong negative affect and neural responses that bias subjects toward defection in subsequent rounds. This suggests a psychological mechanism by which cooperation can unravel in the absence of punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that, without punishment, negative experiences with defectors can lead to reduced cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or payoff, nor does it test the effect of introducing punishment.

- source: Ligon, E., & Schechter, L. (2012). Motives for sharing in social networks. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds high baseline sharing in anonymous, random-recipient dictator games, with small but significant increases in sharing when the dictator can choose the recipient or when the dictator's identity is revealed. The decomposition shows that preference-based motives (baseline, directed altruism) account for most mean sharing, but incentive-based motives (sanctions, reciprocity) explain a large share of the variance in sharing and are strongly associated with real-world gift-giving and social network connectedness. The effect of revealing identity (potential for social sanctions or reciprocity) is modest on average but important for explaining individual differences. The study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a set of dictator game variants, not a PGG or linear public goods game, and there is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism—only the possibility of social sanctions or reciprocity inferred from information structure. The findings suggest that the potential for social sanctions or reciprocity can modestly increase sharing, but the context and outcome measures are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency predictions. The results may be useful for understanding how information and recipient choice affect sharing motives, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Seinen, I., & Schram, A. (2006). Social status and group norms: Indirect reciprocity in a repeated helping experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds strong evidence for indirect reciprocity: donors are significantly more likely to help recipients who have helped others in the past, as measured by the recipient's social status. When information about social status is available, cooperation rates are much higher (e.g., 70% in high-cost/information vs. 22% in high-cost/no-information). Individual strategies often use cut-points based on the recipient's or donor's own social status, and group-level norms about the minimum acceptable status to be helped emerge and are stable within groups but differ across groups. The paper does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but does provide some expected return calculations for different strategies. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; 'punishment' is indirect, in the sense that donors withhold help from low-status recipients.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effects of information and social norms on cooperation in repeated helping games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The design does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but the withholding of help from low-status individuals functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the rate of helping (cooperation), not efficiency or group payoff, though some expected returns are calculated. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that information about others' past behavior and the emergence of group norms can sustain high cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the efficiency effects of explicit punishment mechanisms. The design dimensions most relevant here are information availability, cost/benefit structure, and group size, but the absence of explicit punishment and the focus on individual rather than group payoffs limit direct applicability.

- source: West, S. A., Gardner, A., Shuker, D. M., Reynolds, T., Burton-Chellow, M., Sykes, E. M., Guinnee, M. A., & Griffin, A. S. (2006). Cooperation and the scale of competition in humans. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that human participants cooperate more frequently in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games when rewards are based on global competition (top scorers in the whole class) than when rewards are based on local competition (top scorer in each small group). This effect is robust across individuals, groups, and classes. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the scale of competition (local vs global) can strongly affect cooperation rates in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does highlight the importance of competitive structure as a moderator of cooperation. The findings suggest that contextual factors like competition scale may interact with other mechanisms (such as punishment) in affecting cooperation, but no direct evidence is provided for punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Terai, S., Kiyonari, T., Mifune, N., & Kanazawa, S. (2007). The social exchange heuristic - Managing errors in social exchange. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in one-shot PDGs is significantly higher when participants perceive themselves to be in an ongoing social exchange relationship (i.e., when the SEH is activated) compared to when they do not. In Experiment 1, participants who deliberated about the game and their partner before being assigned to a specific exchange relationship (delayed-play) cooperated less than those who made decisions immediately after being assigned a partner (immediate-play). In Experiment 2, participants who made their contribution decision after being told they were matched with a specific partner (partner-specified) contributed more than those who made their decision before being matched (partner-unspecified). The study attributes these effects to the activation of the SEH, which frames the game as an Assurance Game and increases conditional cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency. However, it does provide informative evidence that cues of social exchange and perceived interdependence can substantially increase cooperation rates in dyadic social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that psychological framing and the presence of cues indicating ongoing relationships may be important moderators of cooperation, but the paper does not address punishment or efficiency directly.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2006). Image scoring and cooperation in a cleaner fish mutualism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that client fish preferentially associate with cleaner fish that are observed to behave cooperatively, providing evidence for image scoring in a non-human animal. Cleaner fish, in turn, behave more cooperatively (feed against their preference) when their actions are being observed by a potential client (image-scoring scenario) compared to when they are not observed. This supports the hypothesis that reputation-based partner choice can promote cooperation. However, the study does not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the experimental setup is not a public goods game but an asymmetric mutualism with partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms (image scoring) can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric mutualisms, which may be relevant for understanding the effects of reputation or partner choice in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The findings may inform predictions about the role of observation and reputation in promoting cooperation, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bandiera, O., Barankay, I., & Rasul, I. (2005). Cooperation in collective action. *Economics of Transition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in a workplace setting with relative incentives (where individual effort imposes a negative externality on co-workers), workers cooperate more (i.e., reduce their productivity) when in smaller groups, with a higher share of co-nationals, with more homogeneous ability, and with more co-workers who have more to lose from shirking. These group characteristics do not affect productivity under piece rates, where there is no incentive to cooperate. The literature review highlights that institutions enabling monitoring, communication, and punishment can increase cooperation in public goods and common pool resource settings, but monetary punishment often reduces group welfare due to its cost. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment and group characteristics on cooperation in collective action settings, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The empirical results are most relevant for understanding how group size, heterogeneity, and incentive structure affect cooperation (proxied by productivity), rather than efficiency. The literature review suggests that punishment can increase cooperation but may reduce efficiency due to its cost, and that communication and monitoring are important moderators. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's findings should be used as indirect signals about the likely direction of cooperation and the possible tradeoff between increased cooperation and the cost of punishment.

- source: Bshary, R., Grutter, A. S., Willener, A. S. T., & Leimar, O. (2008). Pairs of cooperating cleaner fish provide better service quality than singletons. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Pairs of cleaner fish provide higher service quality to clients than singletons, mainly because females behave more cooperatively in pairs. Theoretical modeling shows that cooperation in pairs can yield higher service quality if there is added search efficiency, and empirical data (field and lab) support this. Aggression by males toward cheating females may function as a form of punishment, but this is not systematically manipulated or measured as a treatment. The outcomes are behavioral (cheating, cooperation, aggression), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that, in a biological system with prisoner's dilemma-like structure, the presence of a partner (pair vs singleton) can increase cooperative behavior and service quality, especially when there is some mechanism for partner control (e.g., aggression/punishment). However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is only adjacent to the prediction task and should be used as weak, indirect support for the idea that social context and partner control can moderate cooperation and service quality.

- source: Hill, K. (2002). Altruistic cooperation during foraging by the ache, and the evolved human predisposition to cooperate. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: Ache men and women spend about 10% of their foraging time in cooperative acts that primarily benefit others, with some days exceeding 50%. Most cooperation is directed toward helping others acquire game, and the acts are often short-term altruistic. Cooperation is not limited to kin and is linked to widespread food sharing. The paper argues that such cooperation and sharing are part of an evolved human predisposition, and draws parallels to findings from experimental economics (including public goods games with punishment), but does not itself test or manipulate punishment, nor does it measure efficiency or group payoff in a controlled game-theoretic sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is an observational ethnographic study of real-world foraging cooperation, not an experimental manipulation of game design dimensions. While it discusses the importance of cooperation, sharing, and the possible role of punishment in maintaining social norms, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing context for the prevalence and forms of cooperation in small-scale societies, and in supporting the plausibility of mechanisms (such as contingent sharing and informal sanctions) that are modeled in public goods game experiments.

- source: HEINSOHN, R., & PACKER, C. (1995). COMPLEX COOPERATIVE STRATEGIES IN GROUP-TERRITORIAL AFRICAN LIONS. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that female lions show consistent individual differences in their willingness to participate in group territorial defense, with some acting as leaders and others as laggards. Laggards are recognized by leaders, who become more cautious in their presence but do not punish them or withdraw cooperation. Some laggards adjust their participation based on the odds of group success, while others do not. The diversity of strategies suggests that cooperation in large groups is more complex than predicted by simple reciprocity or punishment models. No evidence of punishment or sanctioning of laggards is found.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the maintenance of cooperation in group settings without formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It suggests that in naturalistic, high-stakes group conflicts, cooperation can persist with a mix of unconditional and conditional cooperators and laggards, and that punishment is not always used to enforce cooperation. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency/payoff outcomes, it offers only adjacent, qualitative insight for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It highlights the possibility that cooperation can be stable without punishment, but does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johnson, T., McElreath, R., & Smirnov, O. (2007). Egalitarian motives in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that people are willing to pay to reduce or augment others' incomes even when there is no cooperative norm to enforce and no material gain from doing so. Both costly taking (punishment-like) and costly giving (reward-like) are common, and are strongly targeted by relative income: high earners are penalized, low earners are rewarded. Negative emotions towards high earners increase with inequality and predict more costly taking and giving. The design randomizes group composition and income, ensuring that actions cannot be motivated by reciprocity or reputation. The study provides evidence for egalitarian motives in income-altering behavior, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods or cooperation context.
